## Exact arithmetic and document check

Run this extraction from the repository root under the environment prefix:

```sh
python3 -I -B - <<'PY'
from pathlib import Path
p = Path('docs/notes/2026-11-02-contract-verification.md')
source = p.read_text().split('```python\n', 1)[1].split('\n```', 1)[0]
exec(compile(source, str(p), 'exec'))
PY
```

The arithmetic is in Q[z]/(z^4+1), z=exp(i*pi/4), with rational
coefficients; H uses (z−z³)/2. There are no floating-point quantum
calculations in this check. Equality of every branch operator to V/sqrt(2)
establishes the resulting channel on all matrix units, hence also with
an external reference; the negative vectors compare density matrices.
This is exact finite mathematical verification, not a producer certificate
accepted by an implementation of the proposed contract.

```python
from fractions import Fraction as F
from pathlib import Path
from math import gcd
import hashlib
import re

def scalar(n):
    return (F(n), F(0), F(0), F(0))

ZERO, ONE = scalar(0), scalar(1)
Z = (F(0), F(1), F(0), F(0))

def add(a, b):
    return tuple(x + y for x, y in zip(a, b))

def neg(a):
    return tuple(-x for x in a)

def mul(a, b):
    out = [F(0)] * 7
    for i in range(4):
        for j in range(4):
            out[i+j] += a[i] * b[j]
    for k in range(6, 3, -1):
        out[k-4] -= out[k]
    return tuple(out[:4])

def conj(a):
    out = [a[0], F(0), F(0), F(0)]
    for k in range(1, 4):
        out[4-k] -= a[k]
    return tuple(out)

I = mul(Z, Z)
TINV = conj(Z)
HFACTOR = (F(0), F(1, 2), F(0), F(-1, 2))
assert mul(HFACTOR, HFACTOR) == scalar(F(1, 2))
assert mul(Z, TINV) == ONE
assert mul(I, I) == neg(ONE)

def compact(state):
    return {i: a for i, a in state.items() if a != ZERO}

def accumulate(state, i, a):
    state[i] = add(state.get(i, ZERO), a)

def enabled(i, controls):
    return all((i >> c) & 1 for c in controls)

def hadamard(state, wire, controls=()):
    out = {}
    mask = 1 << wire
    for i, a in state.items():
        if not enabled(i, controls):
            accumulate(out, i, a)
            continue
        a = mul(a, HFACTOR)
        low = i & ~mask
        accumulate(out, low, a)
        accumulate(out, low | mask, neg(a) if i & mask else a)
    return compact(out)

def flip(state, target, controls=()):
    out = {}
    for i, a in state.items():
        j = i ^ (1 << target) if enabled(i, controls) else i
        accumulate(out, j, a)
    return compact(out)

def phase(state, target, factor, controls=()):
    return compact({i: mul(a, factor) if (i >> target) & 1
                    and enabled(i, controls) else a
                    for i, a in state.items()})

# Bit positions: a=0, b=1, g=2, y=3, outer q=4.
C = [('h', 2), ('t', 2), ('cx', (0, 2)), ('tdg', 2),
     ('cx', (1, 2)), ('t', 2), ('cx', (0, 2)),
     ('tdg', 2), ('h', 2), ('sdg', 2)]
adj = {'h': 'h', 'cx': 'cx', 't': 'tdg', 'tdg': 't',
       's': 'sdg', 'sdg': 's'}
CDAG = [(adj[op], args) for op, args in reversed(C)]

def sequence(state, gates, controls=()):
    for op, args in gates:
        if op == 'h':
            state = hadamard(state, args, controls)
        elif op == 'cx':
            state = flip(state, args[1], (args[0],) + controls)
        else:
            factor = {'t': Z, 'tdg': TINV, 's': I, 'sdg': neg(I)}[op]
            state = phase(state, args, factor, controls)
    return state

for a in range(2):
    for b in range(2):
        i = a | (b << 1)
        assert sequence({i: ONE}, C) == {i | ((a*b) << 2): ONE}
        dirty_phase = I if (a, b) == (0, 1) else neg(I)
        assert sequence({i | 4: ONE}, C) == {
            i | ((1 ^ (a*b)) << 2): dirty_phase}

def expected(state, controlled, closed):
    outer = (4,) if controlled else ()
    state = flip(state, 3, (0, 1) + outer)
    return hadamard(state, 0, outer) if closed else state

def branches(state, controlled=False, closed=False, correction='correct'):
    outer = (4,) if controlled else ()
    state = sequence(state, C, outer)
    state = flip(state, 3, (2,) + outer)
    if closed:
        state = hadamard(state, 0, outer)
    state = hadamard(state, 2)  # Unconditional X-basis measurement.
    result = []
    for m in range(2):
        branch = {i: a for i, a in state.items() if ((i >> 2) & 1) == m}
        if m:
            branch = flip(branch, 2)  # Reset measured private wire.
            transport = closed and correction != 'stale'
            if transport:
                branch = hadamard(branch, 0, outer)
            if correction != 'none':
                controls = (0, 1) + (() if correction == 'uncontrolled' else outer)
                branch = {i: neg(a) if enabled(i, controls) else a
                          for i, a in branch.items()}
            if transport:
                branch = hadamard(branch, 0, outer)
        result.append(compact(branch))
    return result

branch_columns = 0
for controlled in (False, True):
    outer = (4,) if controlled else ()
    for i in range(32):
        if i & 4:
            continue
        start = {i: ONE}
        unitary = sequence(start, C, outer)
        unitary = flip(unitary, 3, (2,) + outer)
        unitary = sequence(unitary, CDAG, outer)
        assert unitary == expected(start, controlled, False)
        for closed in (False, True):
            want = {j: mul(a, HFACTOR)
                    for j, a in expected(start, controlled, closed).items()}
            for branch in branches(start, controlled, closed):
                assert branch == compact(want)
                branch_columns += 1
assert branch_columns == 128

def density(states):
    out = {}
    for state in states:
        for i, a in state.items():
            for j, b in state.items():
                accumulate(out, (i, j), mul(a, conj(b)))
    return compact(out)

superposition = {0: ONE}
for wire in (0, 1, 4):
    superposition = hadamard(superposition, wire)
for controlled, closed, correction in [
        (False, False, 'none'), (True, False, 'uncontrolled'),
        (False, True, 'stale')]:
    actual = density(branches(superposition, controlled, closed, correction))
    ideal = density([expected(superposition, controlled, closed)])
    assert actual != ideal, correction

# Imported model, unit conversion, sequence/parallel accounting.
def model(d):
    return 3*2*d, 2*d*d, F(1, 10) * F(1, 100)**((d+1)//2)
assert model(5) == (30, 50, F(1, 10**7))
assert model(3) == (18, 18, F(1, 100000))
assert F(30, 10**9) == F(3, 10**8)
assert 3*30*50 == 4500
assert (3*30, 50) == (90, 50)  # Sequential time/peak.
assert (30, 3*50) == (30, 150)  # Parallel time/peak.

# Width dispatch, persistent versus reversed load state.
assert (1 ^ 0, 5 ^ 3) == (1, 6)
def later_run(reverse):
    memory, bump = 7, 17  # A stored 7 and allocated at 16.
    if reverse:
        memory, bump = 0, 16
    return memory, bump
assert later_run(False) == (7, 17)
assert later_run(True) == (0, 16)

# Fixed-image coverage; hashing raw bytes differs from hashing hex text.
image_hash = hashlib.sha256(bytes([1, 2])).hexdigest()
assert image_hash != hashlib.sha256(b'0102').hexdigest()
assert 2**(1+1+4) == 64
assert F(64, 2**22) == F(1, 65536)
assert (2**16)*8 == 524288

# Exact modular orbit; no enormous enumeration.
for seed in (0, 3, 4):
    visited, current = set(), seed
    while current not in visited:
        visited.add(current)
        current = (current + 8) % 256
    assert visited == {x for x in range(256) if (x-seed) % 8 == 0}
    assert len(visited) == 32
assert (2**64)//gcd(8, 2**64) == 2**61

# Controlled X and -X produce orthogonal Bell states.
bell_plus = {0: HFACTOR, 3: HFACTOR}
bell_minus = {0: HFACTOR, 3: neg(HFACTOR)}
inner = ZERO
for i, a in bell_plus.items():
    inner = add(inner, mul(conj(a), bell_minus[i]))
assert inner == ZERO

# Fault counts concern code(F_B), not the incoming XOR word.
assert 4*4*16 == 256
assert 1 ^ 1 == 0  # Code 1 failure can leave final fault word zero.
assert 1 ^ 0 == 1  # Successful invocation can leave it nonzero.
assert (1+1, 2*2, max(1, 2)) == (2, 4, 2)  # Root time/occupancy/peak.
assert 1+2 != 2  # Child peaks cannot be summed to get root peak.

# Owned deliverable structure and local links.
base = Path('docs/notes')
ids = ['z4c', 'l2p', 'fd0', 'lpe', 'adg', 'xv4', 'b5b', '5u3',
       'checker-native-model', 'checker-diagnostics', 'checker-fault-rate',
       'checker-resource-contexts']
for ident in ids:

                 'Conformance', 'Release']:

    assert not re.search(r'\b(?:TODO|TBD)\b', body)
    assert sum(line.startswith('```') for line in body.splitlines()) % 2 == 0
    for target in re.findall(r'\[[^\]]+\]\(([^)]+)\)', body):
        if not target.startswith(('https://', 'http://', '#')):
            assert (path.parent / target.split('#')[0]).exists(), (ident, target)
print('PASS: 12 proposals; exact AND 8 columns/32 inverse columns; '
      '128 corrected branch columns; 3 negative channels; '
      'model/domain/orbit/resource arithmetic; fences and local links')

## 5. Regions

### 5.1 Interfaces and common well-formedness

Signatures list roles: `oracle @o (in a: wire[8]) (tgt t: wire[8]) (fault
f: wire[4]) (mem db: region[10,8] in) (args n: i64)`. **Interface
regions** are caller-owned, passed by name, role `in` (returned to entry
contents on every basis configuration) or `mut` (actions only). In the
text form (§16.2) a wire param's role suffix is optional and, when
present, MUST equal its group's role (`in` in an oracle's or action's
`in` group, `mut` in an action's `mut` group); a region param always
carries its role (`in` or `mut`); other region-interface params carry
no suffix. Lproc parameter roles are specified in §3.7 and §16.2. A region
MUST NOT contain `measure effect br call uncall trap hostcall qreg.* budget.mint` or
classical memory instructions, nor lifted rows outside oracle bodies.
Predicate stack empty at entry and exit. Every `qalloc` matched by a
`qfree` of a clean bundle, every `qborrow` by a `qreturn` after its
frame, every local `qregion` restored, by **explicit cleanup
instructions**; the schedule chooses only realisations. **`in` wires in
actions** appear only as controls (including the address operands of
`qload qunload qstore qexchm`, the wires pushed by `qpred`, the selector
of `qif`, whose two `x` gates restore it, and the `cond` wires of `qloop …
while`), as targets of diagonal gates, as `qcopy`/`qxor` sources, as the
value operand of `qstore` (it is only read, as the control of the XOR
into the selected cell), or as `in` arguments of calls. The **region
call graph** — an edge from a function to the callee of each of its
`oracle_call`, `action_call`, `lift.oracle_call`, `lift.oracle_uncall`
and `lift.perm_call` instructions and to both callees of each
`qinplace`, an oracle counting the rows of its lproc closure as its own —
is acyclic (suite F); recursion exists only through `lift.call` among
lprocs.

### 5.2 Lifted oracle

**Body**: lifted and control rows over its locations and lproc closure;
**exact-permutation only** (ε = 0 everywhere, transitively).

**Contract** on every x ∈ D: `ins` unchanged; `tgt` final = initial ⊕
payload(F_B(x)); `fr` final = initial ⊕ code(F_B(x)); `in` regions
unchanged; borrowed bundles returned (§5.6); workspace, locations, local
regions, step state clean / at image. **F_B(x)** = `(f(x), 0)` if the
reference interpreter reaches `lift.retval` within B steps without a
fault (f(x) = the XOR-accumulated `result_val`), else `(0, code)` with
`code` the first fault in sequential order, or `FAULT_BUDGET_STEPS` if
not halted after B steps. The oracle is an **involution** on `(ins, tgt,
fr)` and its own adjoint.

**Construction**: (1) allocate step state (`result_val` included),
locations, local regions, `alloctab` pre-entries, B frames; (2) run B
forward steps; (3) **commit**: `at_HALT = (pc == HALT)`; under `at_HALT ∧
¬faulted`: `qxor result_val tgt`; under `faulted`: `qxor code fr`; under
`¬at_HALT`: XOR the constant `FAULT_BUDGET_STEPS` into `fr`; uncompute
`at_HALT`; (4) **prefix reversal**: run the inverse step for t = B..1,
then release (1). The commit writes persist.

**Domain D**: every value of each `ins`, of `tgt`, of `fr`, of every cell
of every `in` region, of every borrowed bundle; `qalloc`ed wires at 0;
local regions at image.

# spec/

`Bennett-ISA-Spec.md` is the normative text: draft 0.8, **accepted by the
three-model council on 2026-10-28 (round 8, all members accept, no blocking
issues, mean 4.17/5)**. Council records and the runner live in the `bennett`
meta repo (`docs/council/isa-2026-10-28/`, `tools/council.py`).

Rules for changing it:

- Every semantic change bumps the full version string (section 1); drafts
  are not envelope-compatible, so the four example hashes in section 16.3
  change with it. Run `python3 reference-interpreter/isa_check.py --repatch`
  then `python3 reference-interpreter/isa_check.py`.
- Surgical edits only; a rewrite has introduced a mechanical error every
  time it was tried.
- Add every new example or stated result to `isa_check.py` before anyone
  grades it.
- Section 17 lists decisions deferred to implementation with their
  defaults; an implementer who must choose something not covered there
  files an issue and the default goes into section 17 first.
- Revisions go back through the council.

# Checker question 4 — resources for repeated invocation contexts

Proposal for council review; source request 4 in
`../bennett-conformance/checker/.tmp/CROSS_REPO_BEADS.md`.

## Defect and North Star scope

ISA §12.1 enumerates every reached invocation context, including default
coverage; §§11/12.3 allow the same region under different controls,
arguments, budgets and schedule choices. Error entries are common bounds
keyed by variant/control count with explicit multiplicity composition.
But §12.3 region.resources and gates_used are flat maps. §8.2 defines
accounting for an event list, without choosing which invocation's list
these maps describe or declaring a max/sum/common bound interpretation.
K12's estimate must bind the reported resources, so it cannot resolve
this choice either.

This is an S0 contract gap for repeated contexts under rubric C4/C5.
North Star §§4.3–4.4/5.1 requires the full resource vector and
substitutable scheduler/checker interfaces. Ignoring repeated contexts,
rejecting controlled calls or silently taking one context would narrow
the promised machine. An explicitly unchecked flat report is honest
until the contract is repaired, but not a verified resource claim.

## Alternatives and worked witness

Take `@flip(t)` with body `x t`; root `@a(t,c)` executes
`action_call @flip (t) () () ()`, then
`ctl c action_call @flip (t) () () ()`. On a target whose native X
supports total control counts 0..1, duration 1, unit occupancy rate,
the two flip contexts have:

| Context | X count | Time | Qubit-time | Peak |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| forward, 0 inherited controls | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 |
| forward, 1 inherited control | 1 | 1 | 2 | 2 |

The inherited control is live even when its basis value is zero; these
are schedule resource charges, not counts of classically enabled gates.
For the root with both interface wires held throughout two sequential
unit slots, makespan=2, occupancy=4, peak=2 and two X-family native
events. Summing child peaks gives 3, not the root's peak; adding the
root plus its children would count the same leaves twice. These values
are hand-worked under the stated target, not new checker execution.

Even a key of (variant, inherited_controls) is insufficient in general:
different resolved token budgets in nested oracle calls create different
expansion sizes under the same key. Use the full §12.1 context identity.

| Alternative | Assessment |
|---|---|
| Sum every invocation into the region's map | Counts aggregate work but does not define peak/time or let a checker replay individual invocations. |
| Common maximum bound per resource | Could be a separate upper-bound report; is not exact §8.2 accounting and needs a different assertion kind. |
| Pick one context implicitly | Depends on traversal/implementation choice; not reproducible. |
| Exact keyed contexts (recommended) | Reuse §12.1 identity and replay each invocation's event list; root totals remain independently defined. |

## Recommended exact spec diff

Add to §12.3 region records:

```text
resource_contexts?:[{context_key,resources,gates_used,estimate?}]
```

Append after the schema:

```text
For a region reached in exactly one invocation context, resources and
gates_used report that invocation's exact §8.2 scheduled event accounting.
For more than one invocation context, resource_contexts MUST contain
exactly one record for every §12.1 context, in that section's canonical
enumeration order. context_key identifies the actual invocation, not
merely its variant or control count. The checker reconstructs its
arguments, budget, controls, placements, cleanup choices, schedule/default
decisions and native leaves, and recomputes every declared resource and
gate count. An estimate in that record uses §10.1 encodings and binds
exactly that context's values.

For compatibility the unkeyed resources/gates_used/estimate fields remain
the projection of the first canonical context; they MUST equal it and
discharge no other context's obligation. Missing, duplicate, reordered,
foreign or mismatched context records reject the report. A standalone
region with one explicitly bound invocation uses the single-context rule.

A root report counts its realised event list once, including semantic
callee leaves. Child context reports describe their own invocation
subtrees under §8.2 attribution; they are independently checkable views,
not extra costs to add to the root. This rule introduces no second
aggregation by summing child peak/time maps. Error-bound multiplicities
remain governed by their existing separate composition rule.
```

The request's alternative of leaving unkeyed maps semantically unused
would also avoid false verification, but still leaves meaningless data
required. The deterministic first-context projection above pins their
remaining role. Context records are exact reports, not a new optimizer
objective or a claim that every context has identical costs.

## Hashes, catalog and consumers

Certificate bytes and any report digest change. Object closure hashes,
target bytes, schedule bytes and ref catalog need not change. The root
schedule already determines contexts; the schema makes that binding
visible in resource reports. Scheduler, estimator, plugin and checker
must preserve full keys, canonical order and class-qualified K12 keys.
An old multi-context flat report needs recomputation/migration, not an
arbitrary max or sum guessed by the checker.

## Conformance vectors and verification

Use both flip contexts and the root totals above; require control-count
0 and 1 to differ in occupancy/peak. Add two default-covered invocations,
repeated call occurrences, an lproc fixed instance, and differing nested
token budgets under the same variant/control key. Swap context records,
omit one, duplicate one, relabel its call_index, or mismatch its estimate:
reject. A single-context certificate keeps the old map form. The scalar
accounting witness is checked by
[contract-verification](2026-11-02-contract-verification.md); no new
scheduler/checker test was run in this job.

## Release and after acceptance

Recommend a **0.9 K certificate context repair** alongside the fault-rate
binding repair, without changing their distinct meanings. The existing
cross-repo filing request should carry this exact encoding decision.
After council acceptance publish schema and repeated-context fixtures,
then migrate producers and checker independently. This note alone does
not close that obligation.

### 8.4 Space budget

The capacity constraint is peak live qubits per class.

## 9. Objects, identity, linking, reversible ABI (object 0.9)

### 9.1 Sections and schemas

Order fixed: `symbols imports functions data devices layout schedule
certificate coverage`; each at most once; unknown sections or fields are
errors. Metadata sections are one JCS line each, an object:

```
  symbols:  { "<local index>": { "name", "kind": "cc"|"r"|"oracle"|"action"|"perm_action"|"lproc"|"data", "signature": <sig>, "interface_hash" } }
  imports:  { "<name>": { "signature": <sig>, "pinned_closure_hash"?: hex } }
  devices:  { "models": { "<dev>": { "version": "0.9" } }, "services": { "<reverse-dns id>": { "version": "…" } } }
  layout:   { "ptr_width", "H", "A_tab", "S_cells", "null_size", "heap_base", "heap_limit_max",
              "globals": { "<name>": { "base", "size", "image": "<data name>" } },
              "interface_regions": { "<oracle>": { "<region>": { "base" } } }, "block_image"?: "<data name>" }
  schedule: §11      certificate: §12      coverage: { "entries": [ §13 ] }
  <sig>:    kind "oracle": { "tier":"q", "kind", "in":[w…], "tgt":w, "fault":F, "mem":[{"A","W","role"}], "args":[types], "abi":"0.9" }
            kind "action"/"perm_action": { "tier":"q", "kind", "mut":[w…], "in":[w…], "mem":[…], "args":[types], "bounds":[…], "abi" }
            kind "lproc": { "tier":"q", "kind", "params":[w…], "result":w, "tokens":["token"…], "args":[types], "mem":[{"A","W","role":"in"}], "abi" }
            kind "cc"/"r": { "tier", "kind", "params":[types], "results":[types], "abi" }
            kind "data": { "tier":"-", "kind", "size", "abi" }
```

Integers that may exceed 2^53 are decimal strings, and a JSON number ≥
2^53 anywhere in metadata is an error. `data` lines are `NAME SP HEX`.
**Encodings.** `types` in `args`, `params` and `results` are the §16.2
type spellings as JSON strings (`"i64"`, `"ptr"`, `"f64"`, `"token"`,
`"qreg[8]"`); `w` in `in`, `tgt`, `mut`, lproc `params` and `result` is
the integer bundle or location width (§16.3). An lproc's `tokens`,
`args` and `mem` arrays are mandatory (empty arrays when absent from the
header); `args` excludes tokens and `mem` has only `in` roles (§3.7).
Array order is header order within each group. A **program-point id**
(§9.2) is the JSON array `[interface_hash, block name, ordinal]`.
Certificate `frame` fields use exactly the typed-record schema of §9.4;
unknown fields are errors there as in every metadata record.
`devices.models` lists every §7.3 model that an `effect` of the object
uses (an `effect` on an unlisted §7.3 model is a load error; a device
outside §7.3 is never listed and faults at run time, §17 item 3; a key
that is not a §7.3 model is an unknown field); `devices.services` lists
every `hostcall` service (§10). An interface region's name (its param
name and its `layout.interface_regions.<oracle>` key) MUST differ from
every symbol and import name of the object, since `@name` in `lift.addr`
denotes either (§9.2).

### 9.2 Identity

An lproc's token, classical and region parameter groups are part of
its JCS signature (§9.1), including their order and types. The header
and every forwarded operand list are canonical function bytes; changing
them changes the envelope and, through references, the caller's closure
hash. Fixed binding instances are derived per oracle (§3.7), not new
symbols or separately hashed bodies; instance rows share the source
row's program-point id and expand once per fixed row instance per step,
while the instance and semantic-call occurrence components in §11
invocation paths and §12.1 context keys distinguish their uses. [deps: ISA-3.7-P5, ISA-3.7-P6, ISA-11.1-P9, ISA-12.1-P11] Entry argument values and token tuples bind invocations through
`params_hash`, not through the lproc's interface hash (this section).
Imports must match all five groups; validation and instance formation
are repeated after linking (§9.3).

**Canonical function bytes**: the text from the function's header line
through its last instruction line, lines joined by LF, no trailing LF.
**Semantic envelope** of a symbol (byte-exact; every part ends with LF):

```
  (1) "isa=" <full version string, e.g. 0.9>
  (2) "ptr_width=" INT
  (3) the JCS signature
  (4) the canonical function bytes (with reference tokens replaced, below); for a data symbol: its data line NAME SP HEX
  (5a) for every data entry in the closure set, sorted by name bytewise:        "data " NAME " " HEX
  (5b) for every layout leaf in the closure set, sorted by JCS path bytes: "layout " <JCS key-component array> " " <JCS value>. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.1]
  (5c) for an R/CC function, for every device model and service it uses, sorted: "device " <name> " " <JCS entry>
  (6) for every referenced symbol outside the symbol's SCC, in order of first reference: its 64-hex hash
```

**Closure set**, by symbol kind. **Oracle**: **all** `layout` leaves of
the object except `interface_regions` entries of other oracles (every
global's pre-entry affects its allocation faults), its own `interface_
regions.<oracle>.*` leaves, and every data image those leaves name.
**Lproc**: the leaves and images of every global `@g` it names in `lift.
addr`. **Action / permutation action**: no layout leaves; the `@image`
of each `qregion` is an ordinary symbol reference (replaced in part (4),
listed in part (6)). **CC / R function**: all `layout` leaves, every
data image they name, and in (5c) every device model and `hostcall`
service it uses. **Data symbol**: parts (5)–(6) are empty. A layout leaf
is a scalar or an object at the deepest level the schema lists (`globals.
<name>` and `interface_regions.<oracle>.<region>` are leaves; `ptr_
width`, `H`, … are leaves); a scalar's JCS value is its decimal text.
**Globals are data symbols**: every global `g` in `layout.globals` MUST
have `image` = `g` and the object MUST define a `data` symbol named `g`
of that size, so the token `@g` in `lift.addr` is both the global and a
symbol reference (replaced by the data symbol's hash; the global's
`base` and `size` enter through the layout leaf). An `@name` naming an
interface region (in `lift.addr`) is not a symbol reference and is left
unchanged. **Reference replacement**: in parts
(4)–(5) every `@name` symbol-reference token is replaced by `@` followed
by the referenced symbol's 64-hex hash (the symbol's own name in its
header line is a definition, not a reference, and is left unchanged); the **interface hash** uses
interface hashes of defined symbols and `SHA-256(name ‖ 0x00 ‖ JCS
signature)` (hex) for unresolved imports; the **closure hash** uses
closure hashes. **Acyclic and cyclic symbols.** The reference graph
has an edge from a symbol to every symbol it references (parts
(4)–(5)). An **acyclic** symbol — a one-member SCC that does not
reference itself — has hash = SHA-256 of its own envelope (example 1's
`inc8`, example 2's `zero` and `sum_db`); the SCC procedure below applies
to every **cyclic** SCC, including a one-member SCC whose function
references itself (example 2's `sum`). **SCCs**: members ordered by name bytewise; every intra-
SCC `@name` token (parts (4)–(5)) is replaced by `@$<decimal index>`;
intra-SCC references contribute no part-(6) line; `scc_hash` = SHA-256
over the concatenation of the members' envelopes in that order; member
hash = SHA-256 over the ASCII bytes of `<scc_hash hex>:<decimal index>`.
**object_hash** = SHA-256 of the canonical object text with the
`schedule`, `certificate` and `coverage` sections deleted together with
the blank line preceding each. `reference_program_hash` = the oracle's
interface hash. `params_hash` = SHA-256 of the JCS array of classical
parameter values (the typed encoding of §12.3, K13); `schedule_hash` =
SHA-256 of the schedule's JCS bytes.
**Program-point id** = `(symbol interface hash, block name, instruction
ordinal within the block counting nested lines in text order)`.
Executable certificates and caches bind to **closure hashes** (required,
§12).

A layout leaf's path is the JCS array of its original JSON key
components from the layout root to the deepest schema-listed leaf,
so a scalar has a one-component path and an interface-region leaf has
path `["interface_regions","<oracle>","<region>"]`.
[deps: ISA-9.1-P1, ISA-16.1]
Part (5b) emits exactly one line for each structured leaf in the closure
set, including distinct leaves whose dotted renderings coincide, ordered
by the unsigned UTF-8 bytes of that JCS path array, independently of the
leaf values; no dotted rendering is used as a key or an ordering key.
[deps: ISA-9.1-P1, ISA-16.1]
An oracle's own interface-region leaves and entries of other oracles
are selected by equality of the original JSON key under
`interface_regions` to the oracle's name, never by a prefix of a
rendered path; an lproc's global leaves are selected by equality of
the key under `globals` to the global name.
[deps: ISA-3.7-P1, ISA-9.1-P1]

This **aggregation witness** assumes the displayed scheduled intervals,
unit class rates and no other events; it does not assert an unbound
oracle gate count. A permitted cost expression `1 / 3` produces that
makespan. The exact class peaks are 2 and 1, not a replacement scalar 3;

## 11. Scheduling interface (sched 0.9)

```c
typedef struct bennett_sched_request_t bennett_sched_request_t;   typedef struct bennett_sched_response_t bennett_sched_response_t;
bennett_status bennett_sched_request_new(bennett_sched_request_t** out);
bennett_status bennett_sched_request_set_object(bennett_sched_request_t*, const uint8_t*, size_t);
bennett_status bennett_sched_request_set_target(bennett_sched_request_t*, const uint8_t* jcs, size_t);
bennett_status bennett_sched_request_set_region(bennett_sched_request_t*, const char* closure_hash_hex, const char* variant, uint32_t inherited_controls);
bennett_status bennett_sched_request_set_budget(bennett_sched_request_t*, uint64_t steps, uint64_t heap, uint64_t stack, uint64_t depth);
bennett_status bennett_sched_request_set_params(bennett_sched_request_t*, const uint8_t* params_jcs, size_t);
bennett_status bennett_sched_request_set_accuracy(bennett_sched_request_t*, const char* epsilon_decimal);
bennett_status bennett_sched_request_set_capacity(bennett_sched_request_t*, const char* cls, uint64_t peak_qubits);
void           bennett_sched_request_free(bennett_sched_request_t*);
bennett_status bennett_sched_schedule(const bennett_sched_request_t*, bennett_sched_response_t** out);
typedef enum { BENNETT_SCHED_OK, BENNETT_SCHED_INFEASIBLE, BENNETT_SCHED_UNSUPPORTED } bennett_sched_outcome;
bennett_sched_outcome bennett_sched_response_outcome(const bennett_sched_response_t*);
const char*    bennett_sched_response_reason(const bennett_sched_response_t*);
const uint8_t* bennett_sched_response_schedule(const bennett_sched_response_t*, size_t* len);
const uint8_t* bennett_sched_response_certificate(const bennett_sched_response_t*, size_t* len);
void           bennett_sched_response_free(bennett_sched_response_t*);
```

**Generated operations and the expansion library.** Every row and
instruction expands into **generated operations** (gates, allocations,
releases). Above gate level the order is fixed by §3.6, §4.2, §4.5–§4.6
and §5.2 (dispatch comparators k = 0..n in index order; a row's
sub-operations in listed order; successor XORs; commit; cleanup). Below
that level (the gate decomposition of comparators, adders, multiplexers,
`inrange` scans and their scratch) the expansion is defined by an
**expansion library**: a versioned document, identified in the
schedule's `bindings.expansion` as `{ "name", "version", "hash" }`, that
lists for every row kind and every instruction its generated operations
in order, with their **ordinals**, scratch identities and the subset it
marks removable, and that is extensionally equal to the reference (§5.7,
T6). A consumer that does not implement the bound library rejects the
schedule (`E_SCHEDULE_INFEASIBLE`); two consumers implementing it
execute and charge the same operation set. `bennett-isa-spec` publishes
the reference library `ref` (§17 item 7). §5.7 transformations under a
schedule MUST keep the ordinals of the operations they keep and MAY
remove only operations the library marks removable.

**Schedule document** (JCS) has the following schema, including reserved
support points and optional instance/call-occurrence selectors. [deps: ISA-11-P2, ISA-3.7-P5, ISA-11.1-P9, ISA-11.1-P22, ISA-11.1-P29]

```
  { "bindings": { "closure_hash", "target_hash", "region", "variant", "inherited_controls", "budget": [B,heap,stack,depth],
                  "params_hash", "epsilon", "expansion": { "name", "version", "hash" }, "scheduler": { "name", "version" } },
    "interface_vertices"?: [ [v, …], … ],
    "entries": [ { "path": [ { "symbol": hex, "point": "<block>:<ordinal>" | "$construct" | "$step" | "$commit" | "$release", "iteration": [n, …] | "*",
                              "instance"?: [n, …] | "*", "call_index"?: n | "*" }, … ],
                   "step": n | "*",  "phase": "fwd" | "rev" | "*",  "generated": n | "*",
                   "kind": "cleanup" | "gate" | "alloc" | "start",
                   "cleanup": { "realisation": "reverse" | "recompute" },
                   "gate":    { "realisation": "native" | "<scheme name>", "epsilon_share": decimal },
                   "alloc":   { "class": "<cls>", "vertices"?: [v, …] },           /* a concrete class; "any" is not allowed in a schedule */
                   "start":   { "time": decimal } } ] }
```

**Topology placement.** With a §8.3 topology, `interface_vertices`
lists inherited control wires first as individual width-one arrays, then
one vertex array per source interface slot in §9.4 slot order; array
elements follow bit order (and region cell address then bit order). A callee inherits its actual
operand placements. `alloc.vertices`, when present, assigns every bit
of that allocation in bit order. These arrays MUST have the declared
width, resolve to the correct classes and have disjoint live uses;
without a topology they MUST be absent. Omitted placements use the
lowest-index free vertex of the resolved class, assigning bits in order;
interface slots are assigned first in slot order, then allocations in
generated order. Vertices are retained for a wire's lifetime and freed
on release; borrowed wires retain their owner's vertices. Every native
leaf, including scheme leaves, MUST satisfy §8.3 adjacency, else
`E_SCHEDULE_INFEASIBLE` naming §17 item 15. No routing is inserted.

the program points of the region calls and `lift.oracle_call`s that led

### 5.3 Quantum action

**Semantics.** The body denotes a unitary U on mut ⊗ ins ⊗ mem ⊗
(borrowed), where mem includes both `mut` and `in` interface regions,
block-diagonal in the computational basis of `ins` and of `in`-region
cells, identity on borrowed and external reference systems (§5.6),
workspace returned clean, with domain D every value of each `mut`
and `in` bundle, every cell of every `mut` and `in` interface region and
every borrowed bundle, workspace initially at 0 and local regions at
image. [deps: ISA-5.1-P1, ISA-5.2-P4, ISA-3.3-P19, ISA-5.6-P1, ISA-14.2] No fault outcome. Coherent internal
control yields the padded unitary under declared bounds, part of the
action's identity. **Permutation action**: realised unitary a
permutation matrix (ε = 0, permutation gates only, transitively).

**Body**: gates; `qcopy quncopy qxor qexch qinplace`; `qload qunload
qstore qexchm`; `qpred qunpred qif qloop`; `oracle_call action_call`;
`qalloc qborrow qfree qreturn`; `qregion`.
An action body with several `block`s executes its blocks in text order,
each a unitary fragment; labels are descriptive and carry no control
semantics, and lifetimes may span blocks.

**Bounds.** The `bounds { … }` list of an action's `regs` line and the
`bounds` array of its signature hold, in text order, the trip count `n`
of every `qloop` and `qloop … while` in the body (nested loops
included, outer before inner); the list MUST equal the body (suite F)
and is empty for a body without loops. It is part of the action's
identity because the padded unitary of coherent internal control
depends on it.

**Adjoint table** (reverse order, instruction replaced, versions
renumbered):

```
  rx/ry/rz(θ) ↦ (−θ)   s ↔ sdg   t ↔ tdg   x cx ccx mcx swap h cz: self-adjoint
  qalloc ↔ qfree   qborrow ↔ qreturn   qregion creation ↔ release   qcopy ↔ quncopy   qload ↔ qunload
  qxor qexch qstore qexchm: self-adjoint   qinplace @g @ginv tk x (ins) (mem) (args) ↦ qinplace @ginv @g tk x (ins) (mem) (args)
  oracle_call ↦ oracle_call
  action_call @b ↦ adj action_call @b   qpred/qunpred pairs reversed as a whole
  qif w {A}{B} ↦ adjoint of the complete §4.6 expansion, including unconditional lifetimes, in reverse order. [deps: ISA-3.5, ISA-4.6-P7]
  qloop n {A} ↦ qloop n {adj A}
  qloop n while cond {A} ↦ flags computed forward as §4.6; adj A for i = n..1 under c_i; flags uncomputed
```

**Control.** `ctl w @a` is controlled-U: workspace, regions and flags
allocated unconditionally; every gate of the body and its cleanup runs
under `w` in addition to the active predicate.

The shorthand `qif w {adj A}{adj B}` denotes that adjoint when each
arm's allocations and releases are matched within that arm; no
arm-local lifetime restriction is imposed on the source `qif`.
[deps: ISA-3.5, ISA-4.6-P7, ISA-5.1]

Two operations form a **matched pair** iff the second is the §5.3
adjoint of the first, with the same callee(s) (the pair exchanged for
`qinplace`), identical classical argument values and token identities
and tuples, the same operand wires with the first's read-only input
versions and produced target versions current at the second, and equal
effective control sets including the versions of their wires; order and
repetition in predicate stacks are irrelevant. [deps: ISA-2-P10, ISA-3.3-P3, ISA-4.5-P5, ISA-4.7, ISA-5.3-P4, ISA-5.4]
A matched inverse restores preceding wire-version bindings for
subsequent matching only when the §6.2 cleanup conditions hold; matching
alone does not discharge an outstanding-reader or phase obligation, and
a cell write always advances its cell version even when its value is
restored. [deps: ISA-3.3-P3, ISA-6.1-P1, ISA-6.2]

Scheduled action adjoints use the source replacements above before
reference-recipe instantiation, with the ordinal contract and `adj`
adapter specified in §11.1 (K62). [deps: ISA-5.3-P4, ISA-11-P2, ISA-11.1-P29]

# Bennett Virtual ISA — Specification, version 0.9

**Date:** 2026-10-31. **Status:** version 0.9, **accepted by all three council seats on 2026-10-31** (sol r3, codex r2/r4, muse r5; K64/K65 ratified). ISA 0.9 is the version new consumers (bennett-vm M5 onward) implement. ISA 0.8 remains published as `spec/Bennett-ISA-Spec.md` and remains the version the dialect 0.2.x gate and the reference interpreter target until the dialect 0.3 track moves.
Snapshot `59eee9ad0d96` was accepted by the sol and codex seats (council run
`isa-09-2026-10-31`: codex round 2, sol round 3, both core accept, 0 blocking; muse seat held).
K64 and K65 were ratified by the maintainer on 2026-10-31; muse accepted
in round 5 that day, satisfying the council-vote publication gate
(maintainer ruling 2026-10-31). It
revises draft 0.8, which was **accepted by the council in round 8**
(2026-10-28; reports and the editor's responses under
`docs/council/isa-2026-10-28/round-{1..8}/` in the `bennett` meta
repository).
**Round 7: accepted** (2026-11-01, codex seat, 0 blocking). K76 and K77
were accepted by the codex seat in fix rounds 6–7; the three-seat
acceptance of the core stands.
**Round 8: accepted** (2026-11-01, codex seat, 0 blocking).
K78–K80 (passes VIII–X) were accepted by the codex seat in fix round 8;
K1–K80 are accepted in total. The three-seat acceptance of the core stands.
**Round 9: accepted** (2026-11-01, codex seat, 0 blocking). Passes XI–XII
are accepted; K81 was accepted by the codex seat in fix round 9.
K1–K81 are accepted in total. The three-seat acceptance of the core stands.
**Round 10: accepted** (2026-11-01, sol and codex seats, 0 blocking; muse seat advisory
for this one-row fix, pending). K82 is accepted; K1–K82 are accepted in total.
§0 lists every change from 0.8.
Implementation is expected to expose frictions; revisions go back
through the council. Founding text of
`bennett-isa-spec` (`Bennett-NorthStar.md` §4.4, §6.1); the north star
governs where this document is silent. The reference interpreter targets
0.8; 0.9 admission is not claimed. MUST, MUST
NOT, SHOULD, MAY are normative; *(non-normative)* explains; *(deferred)*
names items left open; every deferred decision that an implementer must
take carries a **default** in §17, which implementers follow until the
item is revised.

**Versioning.** ISA 0.9, object 0.9, launch 0.9, sched 0.9, cert 0.9,
coverage 0.9, target 0.9. The **full version string** (`0.9`) is part of
every semantic envelope (§9.2): drafts are not compatible with each
other, so every hash of every 0.8 object differs under 0.9 (§0, §16.3).

---

## 0. Changes from 0.8

Every change from the accepted draft 0.8 has one entry below; nothing
changes without an entry. **Kind**: *E* = editorial (wording, or a
reading pinned that a conformant 0.8 implementation could already take,
with local semantics unchanged); *S* = semantic (some outcome or
well-formedness verdict differs from some reading of 0.8, or a
capability is added). **Hash**: every entry rides on the version bump V,
which changes envelope part (1) and every signature's `abi`, hence the
hash of **every** symbol (§9.2): the four 0.8 example hashes all change
and §16.3 republishes their objects (the 0.8 vectors remain valid 0.8
vectors); "+bytes" marks an entry that also changes the canonical bytes
of functions that use the affected form; "new" marks new symbols.
Sources: *EN* = an item of the evidence note
`docs/notes/2026-10-29-isa-09-evidence.md`; *bd* = a bead of this
repository; *SQ n* = entry n of `reference-interpreter/SPEC-QUESTIONS.md`
(its reading is adopted unless the entry says otherwise); *RC* = the
reference-interpreter council round 1
(`docs/council/isa-ref-2026-10-29/round-1/report.md` in `bennett`),
member and finding; *r8* = a council round-8 suggestion (bead
`b-isa-spec-zsw.N`); *DL* = the dialect 0.2 outline's §25 list.

**No narrowing.** No entry rejects, faults, declares inert or removes a
capability that 0.8 permits or the north star promises. The entries
that add or sharpen a well-formedness check (E11's static view rule,
E16.9, E17.2, E17.3, E17.5's unmatched uncalls, E17.8, E17.9, E17.10,
E17.12, E17.14, the block terminators of K5) concern spellings that
always have a legal equivalent or uses that 0.8 did not define soundly
(E17.14: a location value as a classical argument would be superposed);
the run-time checks (E11, K1's width checks, K3's token validity, K4's
inverse-pair check) concern operations that 0.8 did not define or
already required (0.8's `qinplace` already demanded its certificates;
K4 admits library certificates as well as exhaustive claims, as the
north star §3.1 requires).

# Bennett ISA reference interpreter

Rust 2021, standard library only. ISA 0.8 remains supported; ISA 0.9 migration
is in progress. Complete ISA 0.9 execution is not yet claimed. [PROGRESS.md](PROGRESS.md) records
what is verified, what is staged and in what order; [SPEC-QUESTIONS.md](SPEC-QUESTIONS.md)
records every reading the specification leaves open;
[council/response-round-1.md](council/response-round-1.md) answers the council's
round-1 review blocker by blocker. The crate is not yet a complete conformance
reference: the launch ABI (§10), target descriptions (§8), the schedule
consumer (§11) and the rigorous interval certificate checker (§12.1) are staged
as deliverables 8–11, and claims that need them are reported as unverified,
never as verified.

Build and test offline from the repository root (headless: `DISPLAY`,
`WAYLAND_DISPLAY`, `DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS`, `BROWSER` unset;
`TMPDIR=$PWD/reference-interpreter/.tmp`, created first):

```sh
cargo build --manifest-path reference-interpreter/Cargo.toml --offline
cargo test --manifest-path reference-interpreter/Cargo.toml --offline
cargo test --manifest-path reference-interpreter/Cargo.toml --offline -- --ignored
```

The executable is `reference-interpreter/target/debug/bennett-isa-ref`. Data
commands write one JSON object to stdout and exit 0; `help`/`--help` prints
plain usage. Diagnostics go to stderr with exit status 1, and ISA diagnostics
cite their rule. Integers above 2^53 − 1 are decimal strings (§9.1).

> **Council status (2026-10-31):** accepted by the sol and codex seats in round 24 of run `isa-ref-2026-10-29` on snapshot ad84e75 (461 tests, five exhaustive sweeps; rounds 1–24 under `bennett/docs/council/isa-ref-2026-10-29/`). Staged deliverables 8–11 remain disclosed as staged. Muse seat held.

## Typed R faults and contract migration (ISA-7)

ISA 0.9 already resolves b-isa-spec-221: §0.2 K5, §4.1 `fault`,
§4.9 (implicit cause table, exact 64-bit terminal code, no successor or
later source effect, intact prefix history), §7.4, §10 (FAILED → REVERSED,
no result values, one fault record) and §14.3 T4. Zero maps to code 4;
nonzero codes, including program-defined codes, are preserved exactly.
This is a published 0.9 contract, not an amendment to accepted ISA 0.8.

The dialect 0.2.10 at `023f5a7` §11.5/§17.6 correctly reports
`blocked:BD-B-ISA7` for every R entry targeting ISA 0.8: the mandatory
pre-instruction source step check needs `fault 1` before the instruction,
with the same effect-tape prefix and reversible history. `--budget` here
is host execution fuel, not that source counter; generic `trap` cannot
implement arbitrary typed R faults. Closing the ISA wording issue does
not clear that consumer blocker or certify rung 3. Open follow-up
`b-isa-spec-fuk` retains that producer/executor migration prerequisite.

The reference migration has landed explicit 0.8/0.9 envelope, ABI, identity,
linking and device-version dispatch, plus K5 typed terminal faults and eligible
FAILED-run reversal on 0.9. `tests/typed_faults.rs` checks source-instrumented
pre-instruction boundaries (zero and returns included), exact uint64 codes,
implicit causes, intact prefixes and entry restoration. The source counter
remains producer instrumentation; host fuel is a separate diagnostic.

Bound `ref` expansion, end-to-end K79–K81 execution, the 0.9 saved-tape matrix
and the remaining authoritative delta (including dynamic qregs, minted tokens,
parametrised qinplace and nonempty lproc forwarding) remain open under
`b-isa-spec-fuk.3`–`.7`. The producer and sibling contract pins have not been
migrated here. The full musl hello-world, native equality, R console, reset
and saved-tape replay acceptance remains owed (North Star §8 rung 3;
dialect §19.2). The council status above applies only to its pinned snapshot;
a new milestone acceptance round remains the orchestrator's responsibility.

## Commands

`check <object>` parses and validates the object, computes identities, and
verifies every stored interface hash. Output: `object_hash`, `status`
(`"ok"`, or `"unlinked"` when imports are unresolved), `symbols` (each with
`interface_hash` and `closure_hash`, null when its closure has an unpinned
import) and `unresolved_imports`.

`hash <object>` produces the same identities without comparing stored hashes
(for building fixtures; it edits nothing).

`run-oracle <object> <oracle> --input <x[,y...]> [options]` executes the §14.1
interpreter and prints `{"code":0,"payload":9,"steps":3}` for inc8(8) at B=3.
Inputs of any width are decimal or `0x` hexadecimal; `--input ""` is the empty
list. The oracle name may start with `@`.

Options:

- `--budget B` or `--budget B,heap,stack,depth`. Default B is 1,000,000.
  Layout limits supply heap and stack defaults; depth defaults to 64 with a
  layout; memoryless limits are zero.
- `--mem name=hex` supplies an interface region's bytes in cell order;
  unspecified regions start at zero; the byte count must be exact.
- `--arg value` supplies the next classical argument: an integer,
  `f64:0.5`, or `token:B,heap,stack,depth` (after `--budget` the abbreviated
  token takes its limits as defaults).

`step-check <object> <oracle> MODE [options]` runs the independent §14.1
interpreter and the literal §3.6 machine on each domain element, compares their
F_B, then runs the B inverse steps and compares every location, cell, counter,
stack cell, allocator entry and frame with the entry tuple (T2, T6). The domain
is every input bundle and every byte of every interface region not fixed by
`--mem`; target and fault entry values do not affect the computation (XOR
delivery). MODE is one of:

- `--exhaustive [--max-bits N]`: the whole domain, refusing domains above the
  user's guard N (default 16; no fixed ceiling);
- `--suite`: the §17 item 13 default, exhaustive at ≤ 16 bits and otherwise
  2^16 xoshiro256** samples;
- `--samples N`: N samples, N ≥ 1 (`--seed a,b,c,d` sets the four state words).

Output: `cases`, `input_and_memory_bits`, `mode` (`exhaustive` or `sampled`),
`fixed_regions`, `status`. Default B is 256; `--budget`, `--mem` and `--arg`
apply.

The four commands below share these conventions. A classical value is
`{"type","value"}`: `iN` and `ptr` values are unsigned decimal strings of
their bit-vector values (a signed reading at width N is the caller's), `f64`
is a JSON number, `token` is an object with `steps`, `heap`, `stack` and
`depth` (§9.1 integers), and `qreg` is the register id. Fault records are
`faults`, a list of `{"code","name"}` with the §5.4 code and name (`name` is
null outside the §5.4 table), plus `fault`, that code or null. Tape entries
are `{"args","bytes","dev","op"}`: the §7.3 tape triple plus the bytes the
operation transferred, hex. Measurements are `{"bits","reg","value"}` with
`bits` in wire order (wire 0 first). Draws select the first outcome, in
lexicographic order of the bits string (wire 0 first), whose cumulative Born
weight exceeds draw × total weight; integer values remain little-endian
(SPEC-QUESTIONS 29). Console output is `console_hex` always
and `console` when it is valid UTF-8, else null. `memory_digest` is the
SHA-256 of the complete §7.1 memory (allocation table, heap bump, every
nonzero byte).

For CC/R entries, arguments bind positionally to the entry signature (§10, `E_ARG` on a type
or count mismatch): `--args a,b`, `--input a,b`, or repeated `--arg value`,
at most one form (omission means no arguments). An `iN`/`ptr` value is decimal (negatives accepted within
the signed range) or `0x` hex, range-checked at the width; `f64:` prefixes a
binary64; `token:B[,heap,stack,depth]` is a §5.4-validated budget token
(the four-component `--budget` supplies its defaults, as for `run-oracle`);
`qreg:<bits>` allocates a fresh register and prepares those basis bits
(first character is wire 0). `--entropy hex` is the entropy tape. `--draws
d,...` queues measurement draws in [0, 1). `--replies file` is a JSON array
of `{"service","payload","reply"}` objects (payload/reply hex) consumed in
HOST_REQUEST order: each request must match the next entry's service and
payload, every request must find a reply, and every reply must be consumed,
else the run is a §10 diagnostic and no JSON is printed. `--mem global=hex`
replaces a layout global's image in the entry state, at exactly its size.
For CC/R entries `--budget B[,heap,stack,depth]`'s first component is
execution fuel (CC/R has no ISA step budget; exhaustion is a §3.4
diagnostic, default 1,000,000) and the rest are token defaults.

`run-cc <object> <entry> [options]` runs a CC or R entry on the
`ClassicalMachine` and prints the complete run. Output: `entry`, `status`
(`COMPLETED` or `FAILED`), `steps`, `budgets` (one per `token:` argument, as
for `run-program`), `results`, `fault`, `faults`, `tape`,
`console`, `console_hex`, `clock`, `effect_tape_length`, `memory_digest`,
`host_requests` (`{"address","capacity","payload","reply","service"}`),
`measurements`, and `trace`. Each trace record is one executed instruction:
`step` (counting from 0), `function`, `block`, `index` within the block,
`line` (the object text line), `op`, `lhs`, `operands`, `registers` (each
`{"activation","name","pre","post"}`, the changed dynamic instances with
their previous and new current values, null when absent) and `memory` (each
`{"address","pre","post"}`, every changed byte). A hostcall record includes
its reply's deltas. A fault or trap is a `FAILED` result with no values,
not a diagnostic. This includes runtime region-interface and control/target
aliases created through dynamic SSA: the call records fault 4 before changing
quantum state or consuming a handle. Controls may share read-only inputs.

`reverse <object> <symbol> [options]` runs forward, then reverses, and
reports whether the complete entry state is restored byte for byte:
`equals_entry` and `first_difference` (null when equal, else a description of the first
differing component). `status` is `REVERSED` when equal and `FAILED`
otherwise; a reversal the ISA forbids is a diagnostic citing its rule. The
form depends on the symbol kind. For a CC/R entry, `reverse` takes the
`run-cc` options plus `--steps N` (reverse only after N forward
instructions; without it the whole run); it is §7.4 whole-run or prefix
reversal of the retained history. It also reports `memory_equals_entry`
(the §7.1 memory component). Output: `entry`, the forward `results`, `fault`,
`faults`, `budgets`, `steps`, `tape` and `host_requests`, then the post-reversal
`console`, `console_hex`, `clock`, `effect_tape_length`, `memory_digest`
and `entry_memory_digest`. A faulted prefix reverses like any prefix
(§7.4); a run containing `measure` is a §10 `E_NOT_REVERSIBLE` diagnostic.
For an oracle, `reverse` takes the `run-oracle` options (`--input`,
`--budget`, `--mem`, `--arg`) plus `--steps k` (default B, above B is a
§5.4 diagnostic): it runs k forward steps of the literal §3.6 machine, then
k inverse steps (§14.1 prefix reversal, T2). Output: `oracle`, `budget`,
`payload` and `code` of F_k as read off the machine, `steps` (its executed
rows), `forward_steps` and `reverse_steps` (both k), `frames_clean`, and
`state_after_reversal` with `pc`, `pc_prime`, `faulted`, `counters`
(heap_used, atop, sp, fp, depth) and `locations` (`function.name` to its
bit-vector value). For an action, `reverse` takes the `run-action` options,
executes the action and then its adjoint (§10 reversal of a top-level Q
call), and prints `forward_state` and the post-reversal `state`, `mut` and
`in`.

`run-action <object> <action> [options]` executes a Q action once (its
adjoint with `--adjoint`) on a basis state or a small state vector.

 succeeded in 1002ms:
| `fault` | `fault C` | CC and R; literal 0 ≤ C < 2^64; one terminal record with code C for C ≠ 0, else 4 (§5.4); run ends `FAILED`, no successor executes, prefix history retained for reversal (§7.4, §10, §14.1). One classical instruction (§8.2); in an oracle body or lproc, the one-budget-step §4.2 row instead |
| `qreg.new qreg.free qreg.slice qreg.concat qreg.width qreg.id qreg.from_id` | §3.3 | CC and R (§3.3) |
| `measure` | `r = measure q` / `r = measure.iN q` | CC only; Born rule (§14.2); consumes q; `.iN` for a `qreg[*]` (§3.3) |
| `budget.mint` | `tk = budget.mint @o s h st d` | CC and R; a token for the tuple (s, h, st, d) validated for oracle `@o` (§5.4) |
| `effect` | `effect dev.op args` | R only (§7.3); results are written to memory operands |
| `hostcall` | `rlen = hostcall service ptr len` | CC only (§10) |

**Measurement draws.** Supplied draws are consumed FIFO, one per
executed measurement in dynamic execution order (including measurements
of deterministic states); a failing precondition consumes no draw.
Each draw is in [0,1). Enumerate outcomes lexicographically by their
printed bits string, wire 0 first, and select the first outcome whose
cumulative Born weight is **strictly greater** than the draw times the
total weight. Thus an exact CDF tie selects the next nonzero-weight
outcome. Returned integers remain little-endian (wire 0 is bit 0).
This fixes the reference CLI convention; unsupplied draws follow the
Born distribution (§14.2).

**DRAW-ORDER witness**

```json
{"draws":["0.25","0.75"],"integer_order_values":[1,3],"outcomes_bits":["01","11"],"outcomes_values":[2,3],"reversed_draw_values":[3,2]}
```

Prepare two fresh two-wire registers with H on both wires and measure
them in order. Each has weights 1/4 on `00`, `01`, `10`, `11` in that
order. The first draw selects `01` (integer 2) and the second `11`
(integer 3). Reversed draw consumption would give integers 3,2;
integer-ordered outcomes would give 1,3. Both draws test exact CDF ties.

### 4.2 Lifted rows (oracle bodies and lprocs)

Columns: **Class**, **Reads**, **Writes / slots** (A: displaced values in
slot order; D: the permutation), **Flag / fault** (C = code), **Succ**.
Every row is one budget step; `k` is its own index.

| Row | Form | Class | Reads | Writes / slots | Flag / fault | Succ |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `lift.const` | `w = lift.const.iN c` | A | – | w | – | k+1 |
| `lift.binop` | `w = lift.{add,…,ashr} a b` | A | a, b | w | – | k+1 |
| `lift.div` | `w = lift.{udiv,urem,sdiv,srem} a b` | A | a, b | w (under ¬flag) | flag = (b=0) ∨ (signed ∧ a=MIN ∧ b=−1); C = `FAULT_UB_DIV0` / `FAULT_UB_SDIV_OVERFLOW` | ¬flag: k+1; flag: HALT |
| `lift.icmp` | `w = lift.icmp.{pred} a b` | A | a, b | w:reg[1] | – | k+1 |
| `lift.select` | `w = lift.select p a b` | A | p, a, b | w | – | k+1 |
| `lift.cast` | `w = lift.{zext,sext,trunc}.iM a` | A | a | w | – | k+1 |
| `lift.mov` | `w = lift.mov a` | A | a | w | – | k+1 |
| `lift.parmov` | `(w_1..w_m) = lift.parmov (a_1..a_m)` | A | a_i | w_1..w_m (m slots) | – | k+1 |
| `lift.label` | `w = lift.label ^b` | A | – | w | – | k+1 |
| `lift.addr` | `w = lift.addr @g` | A | – | w = layout base of global or mapped region g | – | k+1 |
| `lift.boundscheck` | `lift.boundscheck p n` | D | p, alloctab | – | flag = ¬inrange(p, n); C = `FAULT_UB_MEMFAULT` | ¬flag: k+1; flag: HALT |
| `lift.load` | `w = lift.load.iN a` | A | the N/8 cells at a.. (§7.2) | w (under ¬flag) | flag = ¬inrange(a, N/8); C = `FAULT_UB_MEMFAULT` | ¬flag: k+1; flag: HALT |
| `lift.store` | `lift.store.iN a v` | A | v | the N/8 cells at a.. (under ¬flag; one slot of N bits) | flag = ¬inrange(a, N/8); C = `FAULT_UB_MEMFAULT` | ¬flag: k+1; flag: HALT |
| `lift.alloc` | `w = lift.alloc n` | A | n, heap_used, atop | under ¬flag, slots in this order: w ← heap_base + heap_used; alloctab[atop] ← (heap_base + heap_used, n, 1, 0); heap_used ← heap_used + max(n,1); atop ← atop + 1 (4 slots, all computed from the old state, then exchanged in this order, `alloctab[atop]` at the old `atop`). **Inverse exchanges**: atop, heap_used, alloctab[atop] (at the restored `atop`), w; then clear the four slots | flag = (heap_used + max(n,1) > heap_limit) ∨ (atop ≥ 2^A_tab); C = `FAULT_BUDGET_HEAP` | ¬flag: k+1; flag: HALT |
| `lift.free` | `lift.free p` | D | p, alloctab | match_i = (i < atop) ∧ (base_i = p) ∧ ¬pinned_i into scratch (at most one i matches: unpinned entries are heap entries, whose bases strictly increase); under ¬flag: live_i ^= match_i for all i; scratch uncomputed from the unchanged atop, bases and pinned bits | flag = ¬∃ i: match_i ∧ live_i; C = `FAULT_UB_MEMFAULT` | ¬flag: k+1; flag: HALT |
| `lift.faultif` | `lift.faultif p code` | D | p | – | flag = p; C = code, written as its effective cause C′ (§5.4) | ¬flag: k+1; flag: HALT |
| `lift.call` | `w = lift.call @f (a_1..a_m) (tokens..) (classical..) (mem..)` (short form: §3.7) | A+D | §3.7 | §3.7 (slots: m capture slots `disp_1..disp_m`, then assignment slots for fp, sp, depth) | flag per §3.7; C = `FAULT_BUDGET_DEPTH` | ¬flag: entry of @f; flag: HALT |
| `lift.ret` | `lift.ret v` | A+D | §3.7 | §3.7 (slots: the delivery slot `disp_1`, then assignment slots for fp, depth) | flag = (depth = 0); C = `FAULT_UB_TRAP` | ¬flag: `succ`; flag: HALT |
| `lift.retval` | `lift.retval v` | D | v | result_val ^= v | – | HALT |
| `lift.oracle_call` | `lift.oracle_call @o tk (ins..) tgt fr (mem..) (args..)` | D | ins, tgt, fr, mem | tgt ^= payload; fr ^= code (callee contract) | – | k+1 |
| `lift.oracle_uncall` | same form | D | as above | the matched inverse (§5.5): the identical permutation | – | k+1 |
| `lift.perm_call` | `lift.perm_call @a (mut..) (ins..) (mem..) (args..)` | D | ins, mut, mem | mut ← P(mut, ins, mem); inverse = `adj` of @a | – | k+1 |
| `qjump` | `qjump ^b` | D | – | – | – | ^b |
| `qbranch` | `qbranch p ^b1 ^b2` | D | p | – | – | p: ^b1; ¬p: ^b2 |
| `qjumpr` | `qjumpr a` | D | a | succ ← a | flag = (a < first_f) ∨ (a ≥ first_f + n_f), where `[first_f, first_f + n_f)` are the row indices of the containing function instance (contiguous, §3.6); C = `FAULT_UB_TRAP` | ¬flag: `succ`; flag: HALT |
| `fault` | `fault C` | D | – | code ^= C′; faulted ^= 1 (C′ the effective cause of C, §5.4) | – | HALT |

**`inrange(p, n)`** = ∃ i < atop: live_i ∧ base_i ≤ p ∧ p + n ≤ base_i +
size_i, every comparison computed at width max(ptr_width, width(n)) + 1
(no wrap; §3.3); `lift.load`/`lift.store` carry this check
**inherently** on their own address and length, so the reference
function is total for every well-formed body; `lift.boundscheck` is an
explicit assertion row the dialect emits before each access (after it
the access's own flag is never set, so dialect-produced bodies' step
counts are unchanged). Rules: `lift.retval` is ill-formed in an lproc;
`lift.ret` is ill-formed outside lprocs; `tk` is a token-typed classical
argument of the calling oracle or a token parameter of the calling
lproc. `lift.oracle_call`/`uncall`/`perm_call` carry the
complete callee interface; `mem` operands name the calling oracle's
mapped interface regions or the calling lproc's region parameters.
`lift.addr @g` names a global anywhere; it names an
interface region **only in the body of the oracle that declares it**
(lprocs are shared between oracles and receive region bases as
parameters, as in §16.3 example 2). Label values obtained by `lift.
label` denote first rows of blocks of the containing function instance;
`qjumpr` to an index outside that instance's row range faults, so
control never leaves a function except through `lift.call`/`lift.ret`; an
in-range index that is not a block's first row enters that row. The
`args` operands of `lift.oracle_call`, `lift.oracle_uncall` and
`lift.perm_call` are classical arguments of the calling oracle or token
and classical parameters of the calling lproc, in callee signature
order and with exactly matching types, never locations. They are fixed
at entry (§3.1, §3.7). Every lifted call available in an oracle body is
also available in an lproc by passing the required token, classical
arguments and mapped regions explicitly through `lift.call`. In lifted rows
a width suffix `.iN`/`.iM` names a location width and has no upper bound
(§3.3), except that `lift.load.iN`/`lift.store.iN` keep N ∈ {8, 16, …,
128}.

**Reference body and binding.** Every oracle carries its **reference
body**: the stream the dialect's versioned mapping produced from the
materialised source; hashed into `interface_hash` (§9.2). F_B and the
budget unit are defined solely by the reference interpreter (§14.1) on
it; the object holds only the reference body; every executor
optimisation is extensionally equal on D (T6).

| Instruction | Form | Rules |
|---|---|---|
| `qalloc` | `w = qalloc cls n` | n clean wires of class `cls` or `any`; unconditional |
| `qborrow` | `w = qborrow cls n` | n wires in unknown state; usable only inside a borrow frame (§5.6) |
| `qfree` | `qfree w` | release a `qalloc`ed bundle clean on every basis configuration |
| `qreturn` | `qreturn w` | matched inverse of `qborrow` after its frame |

every wire's current version is its `qalloc` version (including a
version restored by a matched inverse) or the clean output of an

mod support;
use bennett_isa_ref::{Argument, Budget, Object, OracleInput};

#[test]
fn pass_vii_same_oracle_and_arguments_keep_distinct_invocation_tokens() {
    use bennett_isa_ref::{QuantumBindings, QuantumState};
    let oracle =
        r#"{"abi":"0.8","args":[],"fault":4,"in":[8],"kind":"oracle","mem":[],"tgt":8,"tier":"q"}"#;
    let action = r#"{"abi":"0.8","args":["token","token"],"bounds":[],"in":[8],"kind":"action","mem":[],"mut":[8,4],"tier":"q"}"#;
    let o = Object::parse(&support::object(&[
        ("inc8", oracle, "oracle @inc8 (x:wire[8] in) (t:wire[8]) (f:wire[4]) () ()\nregs { one:reg[8], r:reg[8] }\n^entry():\none = lift.const.i8 1\nr = lift.add x one\nlift.retval r"),
        ("twice", action, "action @twice (t:wire[8] mut, f:wire[4] mut) (x:wire[8] in) () (tk2:token, tk3:token) ()\n^entry():\noracle_call @inc8 tk2 (x) t f () ()\noracle_call @inc8 tk3 (x) t f () ()"),
    ], "{}", "")).unwrap();
    assert!(o.functions["inc8"].params[4].is_empty());
    for input in 0..256 {
        let mut q = QuantumState::new();
        let x = q.allocate(8).unwrap();
        let t = q.allocate(8).unwrap();
        let f = q.allocate(4).unwrap();
        for (wires, value) in [(&x, input), (&t, 0x55), (&f, 0xa)] {
            for (bit, wire) in wires.iter().enumerate() {
                if value >> bit & 1 != 0 {
                    q.gate("x", &[*wire], None, &[]).unwrap();
                }
            }
        }
        let mut bindings = QuantumBindings::default();
        for (name, wires) in [("x", &x), ("t", &t), ("f", &f)] {
            bindings.bundles.insert(name.into(), wires.clone());
        }
        for (name, steps) in [("tk2", 2), ("tk3", 3)] {
            bindings
                .args
                .insert(name.into(), Argument::Token(Budget::steps(steps)));
        }
        let before = q.clone();
        o.execute_action("twice", &mut q, &bindings, false, &[])
            .unwrap();
        assert_eq!(q.read_basis(&x).unwrap(), input as u128);
        assert_eq!(
            q.read_basis(&t).unwrap(),
            (0x55 ^ ((input + 1) & 255)) as u128
        );
        assert_eq!(q.read_basis(&f).unwrap(), 0xb);
        o.execute_action("twice", &mut q, &bindings, true, &[])
            .unwrap();
        assert_eq!(
            q, before,
            "x={input}: inverse must retain both token tuples"
        );
    }
}

#[test]
fn seed_inc8_full_input_domain_and_budget_boundaries() {
    let o = Object::parse(include_str!("fixtures/inc8.bisa")).unwrap();
    for x in 0..256 {
        for b in 0..=4 {
            let r = o
                .run_oracle("inc8", &OracleInput::bits(&[x]), Budget::steps(b))
                .unwrap();
            assert_eq!(
                (r.payload, r.code),
                if b >= 3 { ((x + 1) & 255, 0) } else { (0, 1) },
                "x={x},B={b}"
            );
            assert_eq!(r.steps, b.min(3));
        }
    }
}

# Worked controlled-X action (ISA 0.9, §17 item 7)

`controlled.bisa` calls `flip` under `ctl c`. On the complete domain of
(t,c) it maps (t,c) to (t XOR c,c), with exact +1 basis phase. There are
no allocations, releases, borrowed wires, local regions or classical
arguments. The budget is [0,0,0,0] because neither action invokes an oracle.

The complete published catalog is `spec/ref-0.9.json`, byte-identical to
`spec/ref-0.9.json`: 219 recipes, four-line envelope SHA-256
`5de4e4995350313e50217880ba1b95de39cd043283b726337d4894efb005d197`.
`controlled_action_fixture.py` uses the existing conformance JCS and §9.2
envelope tooling to derive every asset; `--check` compares exact bytes.
The Rust `controlled_action_fixture` test expands the catalog's `ctl`
and `action_call` recipes through the closure-bound flip registry, then
checks every exact matrix column in root (t,c) and callee (c,t) order.
The source registry entry is the one displayed `x t` instruction.

| File | Contents |
|---|---|
| controlled.bisa | Complete 0.9 object with embedded schedule and certificate |
| target.json | Two fast wires, exact native X with one control, duration 1 |
| leaves.json | One X leaf on t[0], effective control c[0], callee ordinal 0, full invocation path, target entry 0, time [0,1) |
| schedule.json | Explicit native selection and start 0 at the callee leaf; action step/phase selectors are both * |
| frame.json | Root mut:0 and in:0 width-one fast slots; empty scratch/history/borrow/ancillae |
| certificate.json | Root invocation, full four-element before/after relation, root and controlled-callee numerical claims with bound 0, both region frames, hashes and composition |
| compat-0.8.bisa | Same source operations under 0.8 identity/schema rules for the pinned interpreter |

The resource vector is time=1, peak_qubits=2, qubit_second=2. Both
interface wires remain live for the one-second gate. Root inherited
controls=0; the flip invocation has inherited_controls=1. The native
leaf and reference are the identical CNOT matrix: V†U-I=0, so the exact
Frobenius bound and diamond error are zero. The root table independently
lists all four complete input/output tuples. The callee frame has only
its mut slot; inherited c is caller-owned, outside its source slots.

The pinned binary is
`/home/tobias/Projects/bennett-workspace/bennett-isa-spec-review/reference-interpreter/target/debug/bennett-isa-ref`.
It accepts ISA 0.8 only (`§1: expected isa=0.8` for 0.9). The precheck runs
its `check` and `hash` on `compat-0.8.bisa`, then all four inputs in both
orientations. That file uses 0.8 frame/table encodings and default ref at
version 0.8, not the published 0.9 binding. CLI `check` validates identity
and metadata admission; it does not replay these certificate claims.
The exact conformance matrix test checks the fixture's mathematical
relation and zero error; neither test claims a pinned 0.9 executor or
interval certificate checker. The binary and interpreter trees are read-only.

# Independent certificate checker

`checker/` is the Rust crate `bennett-cert-checker`. It implements certificate
checking from the published Bennett ISA 0.9 contract, without importing or
copying the reference interpreter, VM or plugin implementation. General-purpose
integer/rational arithmetic and ECMAScript number rendering are dependencies;
JSON parsing, domain resolution, hashing, replay and certificate reasoning live
in this crate.

Legacy certificates, including the published 0.8 compatibility object, are
reported unchecked until their version-specific contract is implemented.

The implementation is in progress. The published `controlled-action-09`
fixture now receives a complete **verified** verdict: its full-tuple exhaustive
claim, both numerical claims, native target feasibility, resources, gate counts,
invocation multiplicities and error composition are checked independently.
This is a verified fixture path, not a claim that every §12 capability has been
implemented. Unsupported obligations still produce explicit unchecked results.

## Running

```sh
cargo run --offline --manifest-path checker/Cargo.toml --bin bennett-check -- \
  certificate.json --object program.bisa --target target.json --schedule schedule.json

cargo run --offline --manifest-path checker/Cargo.toml --bin bennett-check -- \
  --from-object program.bisa --target target.json
```

An omitted `--schedule` uses the object's embedded schedule, if any. A standalone
reference table/program relation does not need a schedule. Explicit documents
are checked against the certificate bindings. The object hash excludes both
schedule and certificate sections as §9.2 requires; changing the schedule still
changes its separate binding.

`--max-elements N` bounds replay and native expansion work. `--max-wires N`
bounds matrix work; the current dense rational implementation also has a
six-wire host cap. Event/resource tracing uses a separate bound of at most
1,048,576 wire identities and can verify wide interfaces without matrix work.
Channel LDL work has an eight-dimensional interface cap,
and interval precision has a 4096-bit host cap. These are implementation
limits, never ISA legality limits. Retained replay/table/coverage storage is
also bounded by a conservative 256 MiB estimate before allocation. `--library` accepts additional JCS documents;
independent library-proof provenance is still unchecked. `--oracle-bin` is
reserved for differential replay and explicitly reports that the optional
comparison has not been performed.

Native source expansion currently permits at most 100,000 expanded rows and
64 loop/invocation levels. Exact decimal materialisation permits at most
100,000 significand digits and an absolute decimal scale of 100,000. Oversized
decimal fields produce an explicit host-limit verdict before constructing
powers of ten; exponents never truncate into machine-sized powers. This
preflight applies to decimal fields, including embedded schedule fields, and
leaves names and other opaque strings untouched.

Every object metadata section must itself contain JCS bytes. The §9.1 rule
rejecting JSON numbers at or above 2^53 applies recursively to metadata in
objects and supplied certificate, target and schedule documents. Exact decimal
strings retain their field-specific interpretation and are not rounded into
JSON numbers.

Reference oracle replay has a separate 1,048,576-row work limit per evaluation,
shared by its nested oracle calls. Each nested call still uses its own ISA
budget. A host work limit gives no proof label, evidence count or coverage;
ISA budget exhaustion at that boundary remains the specified budget fault.

The executable prints exactly one canonical JSON verdict line on stdout.
Usage and file/UTF-8 input errors go to stderr. Exit status is:

| Status | Meaning |
|---|---|
| 0 | Every represented obligation was checked successfully |
| 1 | At least one certificate obligation was rejected |
| 2 | At least one obligation remains unchecked, without a rejection |
| 3 | Usage or file input error |

Output has `overall`, recomputed `hashes`, global `errors`, `establishes`,
`unchecked_items`, and `regions` keyed by closure hash. Each region has errors,
unchecked obligations and claims in their original zero-based order. Diagnostics
contain `code`, `section`, `path` and a concrete message. Output has no timestamps,
random IDs or execution-time fields. Byte-identical inputs yield identical JSON.

## What a claim establishes

| Claim outcome | Established scope |
|---|---|
| `proof` | Exhaustive agreement on every component of D, or both inverse-pair compositions over the entry's full input/memory domain and declared classical-argument ranges |
| `evidence` | Agreement only on the stated slice or sampled draws, with exact replay count and distinct covered fraction |
| `accept` | The named numerical/channel bound or syntactic structural rule at the resolved invocation scope |
| `reject` | A named obligation failed; a diagnostic gives its witness or malformed field |
| `unchecked` | No conclusion; a reason identifies the missing mechanism or host limit |

For exhaustive and sampled relations, complete tuples include inherited controls,
all interface bundles (including arbitrary initial oracle target and fault
contents), all borrowed positions and every interface region cell. Omissions
are zero-filled slices, never full coverage. Components resolve by role/position,
not a union of interface and borrowed names. Expected programs correspond by
signature position, allowing different local names. Inputs and borrowed systems
must be preserved, workspace must finish clean, and permutation replay admits
no phase approximation. A producer's requested proof label is rejected if the
completed check yields only evidence. Sampled success stays evidence even at
full distinct coverage. Host-limited claims have no evidence count or fraction.

Numerical checking constructs the realised and reference matrices at every
resolved native action context, controls first in allocation order. It computes
interval enclosures of `V†U-I` and the Frobenius upper bound, with outward binary
rounding after arithmetic operations. Channel checking constructs the
unnormalised, input-first Choi difference and verifies all three dual inequalities
by interval Hermitian LDL factorisation. Every pivot must have a strictly
positive lower endpoint; zero, negative and inconclusive pivots reject. The
reported bound is the diamond norm itself, not half that norm.

The translation lemma checks the actual X-family circuit, including effective
controls, the exact `V;W;reverse(V);W` shape, live borrowed wires, all-W-control
exclusion and maximality. Its conclusion concerns that reference borrow frame
on arbitrary borrowed states, including entanglement with an external reference.
It does not establish another frame's entry domain or a target realisation.

## Current implementation boundary

Reference replay covers an independently implemented subset of lifted arithmetic
and permutation-action semantics. Unsupported rows, lproc/stack machinery and
unresolved invocation contexts are explicit unchecked outcomes. The native
matrix path currently handles single-class action calls, sequential action
blocks, counted `qloop` bodies, coherent `qif` arms and
X/CX/CCX/MCX/SWAP/H/S/S†/T/T†/CZ/RX/RY/RZ
gates. It includes invocations covered entirely
by defaults, forwards classical arguments, and checks maximal gate selector
specificity and ties. Static source ordinals are retained across loop visits;
iteration selectors use the complete dynamic nesting vector. Zero-trip loops
emit no events and cannot witness a selector tie. The two selector flips of
`qif` retain their own generated ordinals; full complex-column tests check
both arms and global phase. Scheme expansion, `qloop while`, topology, multi-class
allocation and complete oracle expansion remain unchecked.

Rotation literals and bound `f64` arguments are decoded to exact binary64
rationals, including signed zero. Sin/cos use outward rational Taylor
enclosures with a Lagrange remainder below the §17 threshold. Modulo reduction
uses a proved Machin-formula enclosure of π. Excessive cancellation or work
produces a host-limit outcome; it never supplies an approximate constant or
an acceptance tolerance. These claims establish the requested numerical bound,
including global phase, rather than exact equality for arbitrary angles.

The checker does not infer exactness from the mere presence of an epsilon-zero
target entry. It checks the selected native entry with the actual total control
count. A scheme built from exact leaves is not assumed to realise its advertised
operation. Nonzero native epsilon alone does not specify the actual operator;
